[M00779] 2026-08-07T06:40:40.142Z ASSISTANT (conversation) [C007:471; copies=14]
**No, that warning is fatal.** It means the extraction was running on CPU. `probe OK` printed because the model produced keypoints — just not on the GPU.

The chain: `onnxruntime-gpu` 1.28 is a **CUDA 13** build (confirmed in the official table — "starting with version 1.27, GPU packages published to PyPI are built with CUDA 13.0 by default"). Kaggle's image is CUDA 12, so `libcublasLt.so.13` doesn't exist, the provider `.so` fails to load, and onnxruntime falls back silently. Your `requirements-train.txt` floor was `onnxruntime-gpu>=1.18`, unbounded, so it resolved to the newest.

My own guard had the same hole and let it through. `ort.get_available_providers()` reports what the build was *compiled* with, not what *loaded* — it listed `CUDAExecutionProvider` while CUDA was unusable. The probe now asserts on `body.session.get_providers()`, which is the providers in effect:

```
  provider IN USE: CUDAExecutionProvider
```

Three fixes, all in the generator:

| fix | why |
|---|---|
| pin `onnxruntime-gpu==1.26.0` | newest CUDA 12.8 build — same toolkit as torch 2.7.1+cu128 |
| `pip install --no-deps rtmlib` + uninstall CPU `onnxruntime` | rtmlib depends on the CPU package; both own the same module dir, and it overwrote the GPU build (visible at your log line 35: `Installing collected packages: onnxruntime-gpu, onnxruntime, rtmlib`) |
| assert on the live session | closes the hole that let 1.28 pass |

**Second failure: Kaggle auto-extracted your archives.** 01a saved the zips unopened, but the published dataset holds `Charades_v1_480/Charades_v1_480/*.mp4` — 9848 loose files, no `.zip`. My cell only knew the zip layout, so it asserted "not attached" against a dataset that plainly was. It now detects both and prefers extracted: no unpack step, nothing consuming the 20 GB working quota. That's the better path, so 01a needs no rerun.

Also fixed the wheel-directory selection you patched by hand. `attached ['competitions', 'datasets']` — the ARC competition ships its own `.whl` files, and `sorted(...)[0]` picked those. It now scores candidate directories by how many of *our* packages they hold, so no filtering by name.

**You must re-run notebook 00 before 01.** Notebook 01 installs with `--no-index` from the staged cache, and that cache currently holds `onnxruntime_gpu-1.28.0`. Offline it cannot fetch 1.26.0 — so stage it first. Torch is cached, so this is ~5 minutes.

Answering your earlier question directly: **the code dataset only needs re-uploading when `src/`, `scripts/`, `configs/`, or `weights/` change.** Your log line 42 shows it's current — `contract 8/8 - mounted code is current` — and these fixes were all notebook-side, so no re-upload this time.

132/132 tests pass, including the training suite (16/16) which hadn't been run since the restructure.

**Two things to answer while you're in that first 01 session**, since they change the plan materially:

Advance `VID_START`/`VID_END` per run, and check whether the staged RTMO ONNX has a dynamic batch axis. RTMO is called at `batch=1`, which is latency-bound — if the axis is dynamic, batching is worth ~3–5× and could collapse four sessions into one or two. If it's static, four sessions stands and you plan around it.

```

```

That second pair matters as much as the first — OSNet was the gap that would have blocked notebook 03's preflight. It's closed now, which confirms your code dataset re-upload included `weights/`.

**One thing in the log to not be alarmed by, and one to know about:**

`onnxruntime-1.28.0` also got staged (line 1268) — pulled in as an rtmlib dependency, not a mistake. It's harmless because notebook 01 uninstalls the CPU build and installs rtmlib with `--no-deps`, so it can never be selected. Leaving it costs 19 MB and keeps `pip download` from having to fight the resolver.

Line 1366 is worth knowing: the RT-DETR export ran `AutoUpdate` and installed `onnxruntime==1.28.0` **into the notebook 00 session**. It doesn't touch the wheel cache and 00 is online-only, so nothing downstream is affected — but it's exactly the clobber pattern that cost you the last session, arriving from a direction I hadn't guarded.

I added a version assert to notebook 00's final cell so a CUDA-13 build can never reach the dataset again:

```
onnxruntime_gpu-1.26.0-cp311-...whl  (CUDA 12 build, correct)
onnxruntime_gpu-1.26.0-cp312-...whl  (CUDA 12 build, correct)
```

I also corrected the last cell's instruction — it still said "create TWO datasets", which contradicts your single-dataset setup. It now says update the existing one, and explains that the name doesn't matter since resolution is by content.

**Re-run notebook 00 once more before Save Version.** Only to pick up that assert — ~3 minutes, everything is cached. If you'd rather not, the run you already have is fine: the correct wheels are staged, and the assert is protection for future runs, not a fix for this one.

**Then: Save Version → update `behavioursense-WW`** (not a new dataset — a second copy costs quota and gives the resolver two candidates).

```
provider IN USE: CUDAExecutionProvider
```

```
N2d both Charades layouts resolve to the same rows/video_path interface;
    absence refuses naming both
```

The layout cell detects which shape it got, prints `layout PRE-EXTRACTED` or `layout ZIPS`, and exposes one interface (`rows`, `video_path()`) so the extraction cell has no branch. Your case is pre-extracted: no unpack step, nothing in the 20 GB working quota, straight to inference. The refusal control asserts that a genuinely-missing dataset names *both* patterns it looked for, so the next person doesn't get misdiagnosed the way you just were.

**Next step: copy the regenerated `notebooks/01_prepare_adl_shards_offline.ipynb` into Kaggle and re-run.** This is the step that bit you — the Kaggle copy is a snapshot, and it was three fixes behind. The repo contract catches a stale *code dataset*; it can't see a stale notebook.

[M00810] 2026-08-07T10:45:32.231Z ASSISTANT (conversation) [C007:547; copies=13]
Yes, fine. Identical to the papermill run — same three warnings, and the line that matters is there:

```
provider IN USE: CUDAExecutionProvider
```

The only difference is ordering. Those warnings go to **stderr**, the prints to **stdout**, and interactive Jupyter flushes the two independently — so they surface after the probe output instead of before it. Same events, different interleaving.

On the warnings themselves: ORT assigns shape/NMS ops to CPU on purpose, and the 10 Memcpy nodes are the host↔device copies at those boundaries. It's inherent to the RTMO export, not something your setup caused. The practical cost is a small constant per frame, which is already inside the "batch=1, latency-bound, expect ~2–3× a T4" estimate in the plan — not a separate problem to chase.

Go ahead and run the rest. What to watch:

```
layout PRE-EXTRACTED (Kaggle unpacked the archives)
  3000/3000 slice ids resolve to a readable mp4
```

then the loop's `N/3000 videos (X/h, ETA Y h)` every 100 videos. Check the first ETA against your remaining session time — if 3000 won't fit, stop the loop early and Save Version anyway. Partial slices are fine; the carry-forward cell accumulates them, and the shard files already written stay valid. **Classes 7/8 empty is correct** — Charades has no falls. They come from OmniFall via notebook 02 and train as a separate binary head. Verified the guards: `class_balanced_sampler` gives absent classes zero weight, `evaluate()` masks them from macro-F1.

**Classes 1 and 6 are the real finding.** Charades has no verb for a bare posture — reaching and placing appear only as *"Putting a box somewhere"*. I first wrote this up as a mapping oversight, then checked the source: `build_charades_map.py:14` documents routing that whole family to `other_idle` **deliberately**, because it "carries no signal any behavioural feature consumes." I verified that claim rather than trusting it — no entry in `TRACKED_FEATURES` reads class 1 or 6. So the behaviour layer is genuinely unaffected, and re-extracting for 6.3 h would buy nothing.

But it does cost macro-F1, and nothing in the output would have explained why. That was the actual defect, so I fixed the reporting:

- `train_adl.py` and notebook 04 now emit **`macro_f1`** (all present classes) **and `macro_f1_supported`** (≥50 val windows), plus per-class F1 with support
- starved classes are **named** at the end of training and under the P1 table, with the cause

Validated against your exact distribution: the gap is **+0.043 macro-F1** from one class at 13 val windows. `standing` clears the floor and stays in both averages with near-zero F1 — the threshold reports honestly rather than flattering the number. Both columns belong in the dissertation.

**Yes — and now the upload is required *before* notebook 02, not just before 03.** Last message I told you 02 didn't need it. That was true then; fixing the fall labels made it false, because notebook 02 now calls `window_clip(..., with_starts=True)` and your uploaded copy doesn't have that parameter.

Your header pasted above is what prompted the check, and it doesn't match the code. It advertises four corpora — Le2i, CAUCAFall, GMDCSA-24, URFD. Only GMDCSA-24 has a live URL; the other three are commented out pending private uploads. That matters beyond bookkeeping: **P2 is leave-one-dataset-out, and one corpus cannot support it.** The notebook now prints that instead of letting notebook 04 report a single-fold number as if it were a result.

Two real defects found in the labelling, both in the only label source the fall head has:

**Labels tracked list index instead of time.** `window_clip` drops low-visibility windows, so `enumerate()` is not a temporal position. With 3 s of occlusion at the head of a 10 s clip, the descent label landed 2 s late:

```
no occlusion : 9 windows, 'falling' at frame 60
3 s occluded : 7 windows, 'falling' at frame 75   <- true midpoint is 75
```

Before the fix, the real descent was labelled `standing` and a standing window was labelled `falling`. In a fall detector, that's the worst place for a silent label error.

**Some clip lengths produced zero `falling` windows.** The band `0.4 <= frac < 0.6` misses when the fraction grid steps over it — a 5 s clip gives `{0, .33, .67, 1}`. Fall corpora are mostly short clips, so the safety-critical class was being starved by arithmetic. Every length from 3 s to 15 s now yields at least one.

Also added a shard gate, because notebook 01 taught this lesson: it refuses to publish if there are no windows, no falls, or no non-falls. A binary head trained on one class reports AUROC ≈ 0.5 and nothing in the metric says the *data* was degenerate rather than the model bad.

`N15` locks all of it and fails if the notebook ever reverts to `enumerate(wins)`. **134/134 tests pass** (18 notebook, 16 training). Regeneration is deterministic.

**Do this, in order:**

```bash
kaggle datasets version -p . --dir-mode zip -m "fall labels by frame position + shard gate"
```

[M00887] 2026-08-07T19:45:48.217Z ASSISTANT (conversation) [C007:833; copies=13]
Everything's wired correctly. Here's the direct answer to your question.

**Only GMDCSA-24 extracts automatically. The other three don't extract at all right now** — they're commented-out placeholders in cell 4.

I verified GMDCSA-24 against the real repo via the GitHub API rather than trusting the URL:

```
160 videos, 1.11 GB, no Git LFS
Subject 1/ADL   16    Subject 1/Fall   16
Subject 2/ADL   23    Subject 2/Fall   25
Subject 3/ADL   22    Subject 3/Fall   21
Subject 4/ADL   20    Subject 4/Fall   17
                      → 79 fall / 81 ADL
```

`git clone --depth 1` gets the videos directly, so extraction works. **Le2i, CAUCAFall, URFD have no reliable public mirror** — you'd download them manually, upload each as a private Kaggle dataset, then uncomment the matching line in `SOURCES`. Until then, **P2 leave-one-dataset-out cannot run**; it needs ≥2 corpora, and the notebook now prints that instead of letting notebook 04 report one fold as a result.

Reading the layout exposed a bug that mattered more than the question:

**Subject ids collapsed four people into one.** Every GMDCSA subject numbers its clips `01`–`25`, so `f"{stem.split('_')[0]}"` mapped `Subject 1/ADL/01.mp4` and `Subject 2/ADL/01.mp4` to the same `gmdcsa_01`. `split_by_subject` holds ids out — so one id spanning four people puts **every person on both sides of the split**, and the cross-subject number for the safety-critical head becomes memorisation reported as generalisation. The disjointness assert can't catch it: the ids really are disjoint, the people behind them aren't. Now derived from the directory (`subject_from_path`), verified across all four corpora's shapes, and the gate prints every id with its window count and refuses fewer than two.

**The label lives in the directory, not the filename** — every clip is `01.mp4`. The old rule worked here by accident via the `Fall/` parent; `is_fall_clip()` now walks path components and prefers explicit `ADL`/`Fall` markers.

**Separately, a test-suite failure surfaced mid-run and was real.** `connect(path=DB_PATH)` bound the default at definition time, so the test fixture's redirect did nothing and every run wrote to the committed `data/behaviorsense.db`. Row counts grew 2 per run (58→60→62→64→66) until they crossed `?limit=50` — after roughly 40 runs of passing while proving nothing about isolation. Fixed both ends: `connect()` resolves at call time, and `fresh_client()` now *asserts* the redirect landed via `PRAGMA database_list`. True count is 2 alerts from one injected fall; the other 64 rows were debris. Stale DB deleted.

134/134 tests pass.

[M00889] 2026-08-07T19:57:20.217Z ASSISTANT (conversation) [C022:1035; copies=1]
**T4 x2** (or P100 — same outcome). Not the Blackwell.

Notebook 02 needs internet for the GMDCSA clone, and Kaggle's internet-enabled GPU sessions are T4/P100. The Blackwell runs with internet off, so it can't fetch the corpus at all. That decides it before performance enters the picture.

Performance wouldn't change the answer anyway. Sizing the job against your measured notebook-01 rate (10.7× realtime, 7,985 videos in 6.26 h):

```
160 videos, ~8-20s each  ->  2-5 min inference on Blackwell
                         ->  5-12 min on a T4
fixed cost: clone 1.11 GB + pip + model load  ~4-5 min
```

Roughly a 10-minute session either way. The fixed costs dominate, so the faster card buys you a couple of minutes and spends Blackwell quota you need for notebook 03's 12-hour training runs.

**Settings:** Accelerator **GPU T4 x2**, Internet **ON**. The notebook only uses one GPU — T4 x2 is just what Kaggle offers; nothing to configure.

One thing to watch on cell 3: the `CUDAExecutionProvider` assert. It's there because onnxruntime silently falls back to CPU when the CUDA provider is missing, which turns this into a multi-hour job that looks merely slow rather than misconfigured. If it fires on a T4, the accelerator didn't attach — fix the setting, don't remove the assert.

For contrast, the accelerator per notebook:

| notebook | accelerator | internet |
|---|---|---|
| 00 stage assets | **None** (CPU) | ON |
| 01a stage Charades | **None** (CPU) | ON |
| 01 ADL shards | Blackwell | **OFF** |
| **02 fall shards** | **T4 x2** | **ON** |
| 03 train | Blackwell | **OFF** |
| 04 evaluate | Blackwell | **OFF** |

00 and 01a are pure downloads — a GPU there is quota burned on I/O, which is the waste that moved extraction offline in the first place.

## CAUCAFall — automatic, no upload

Mendeley DOI `10.17632/7w7fccy7ky.4`, CC BY 4.0, 10 subjects × 10 activities (5 fall types + 5 ADLs). Worth knowing: it's `1080×960 @ 23 fps` from an IR camera in an upper wall corner — a genuinely different viewpoint from GMDCSA's, which is what makes leave-one-dataset-out meaningful rather than four samples of the same setup.

## Le2i — this one you upload

No canonical host survives. Charfi et al. (2012) described 250 videos; redistributions disagree with each other and with the paper (one lists `home_1: 30, home_2: 30, Office: 33, coffee_room_1: 48`, another the four-scene 60/70/64/27 split). Only **Home** and **Coffee room** ship `Annotation_files` with fall start/end frames, which is why most work uses just those two.

Get it from a GitHub mirror — [YifeiYang210/Fall_Detection_dataset](https://github.com/YifeiYang210/Fall_Detection_dataset) documents the four-scene breakdown, [zhoukaisspu/Human-Falling-Detect-Tracks](https://github.com/zhoukaisspu/Human-Falling-Detect-Tracks) covers Coffee room + Home, [Vuong02011996/action_recognition](https://github.com/Vuong02011996/action_recognition) notes the annotation limitation. Then:

```bash
kaggle datasets create -p ./le2i --dir-mode zip
```

Keep the scene folders intact (`Home/`, `Coffee_room/`, …) — `subject_from_path` derives subject ids from the directory layout, and flattening them recreates exactly the collapse I fixed yesterday.

## On the shared quota

You're right, and my earlier reasoning was wrong: with one pool, the fastest card costs the *least* quota per unit of work, so "save the Blackwell" was backwards. T4 x2 for notebook 02 still holds, but only because the Blackwell has no internet.

That makes **notebook 03 the real quota question**, not 02. Two corpora instead of one roughly doubles the fall-head data, and P2 leave-one-dataset-out needs a fold per corpus — three corpora means three training runs of the fall head. Worth deciding deliberately before you spend hours on it.

Two caveats worth stating before you rely on it:

**Licence is "Unknown."** Le2i's canonical host (`le2i.cnrs.fr`) is dead — connection refused — and the IMVIA successor page 404s. (2012) as the source and note the mirror as the access path. Keep your derived shards private regardless — that's the same discipline you're already applying to Charades.

**The size reported is inconsistent** — 10.06 GB from the search endpoint, 17.44 GB from the view endpoint. Probably versions differing, but it means the mount contents shouldn't be assumed. The notebook will discover the layout by walking the tree rather than hard-coding paths, same as the content-based resolution everywhere else.

**Not before — now yes, all four are automatic.** I tested the label rules against each corpus's real layout first, and two would have failed silently:

```
caucafall  Subject.1/Fall forward/video.avi    want True  got False  BAD
caucafall  Subject.1/Fall backward/v.avi       want True  got False  BAD
le2i       Coffee_room_01/video (1).avi        no marker anywhere    ??
```

**CAUCAFall** — the rule tested `part in FALL_DIR_MARKERS`, an *exact* match, but its folders are `Fall forward`, `Fall backward`, `Fall left`. All 50 fall clips would have been labelled ADL — feeding real falls to the binary head as negatives. Now token-matched, ADL checked first so "no fall" can't read as "fall".

**Le2i** — worse, because it has no marker in any path component. Every clip is `video (N).avi` under a room folder; the truth lives in `Annotation_files/*.txt`, first two lines being the fall's start and end frame. Defaulting to "not a fall" would have buried its ~192 fall clips in the negatives. Unreadable annotations are now **skipped and counted, never guessed**.

That turned into an accuracy gain: where the annotation exists it beats the heuristic outright — real frame boundaries instead of "the descent is somewhere mid-clip":

```
Le2i, fall annotated at frames 60-90:
  stand stand stand FALL FALL FALL FALL fallen fallen fallen fallen
```

One conversion matters and is asserted: annotations are in **original** frame numbers, windows in **subsampled** ones, so the interval is divided by the decode step. Without it the fall interval is ~2× wrong at 15 fps.

**How each is acquired** (all verified against live hosts today, not assumed):

| corpus | route | note |
|---|---|---|
| GMDCSA-24 | `git clone --depth 1` | 160 videos, 1.11 GB, no LFS |
| URFD | 70 direct MP4s | the host every paper cites is dead; `fenix.ur.edu.pl` works |
| CAUCAFall | Mendeley file-API walk | bulk S3 zip is 403, root listing is figures only |
| **Le2i** | **attach `tuyenldvn/falldataset-imvia`** | no surviving origin — mirror is the only route |

Le2i is the one thing you attach, and it's a mount rather than an upload — no download, no storage quota. The notebook finds it by content (a directory holding *both* video and annotations), so any mirror or title works, and a video-only mirror is refused loudly rather than labelled entirely ADL.

Four corpora is what makes **P2 leave-one-dataset-out** real: each fold holds out a genuinely different setup — phone video, Kinect at 1 m, IR camera in a wall corner at 1080×960, 320×240 across four rooms. With one it was a formality.

135/135 tests pass (`N16` locks all nine marker cases plus the four Le2i annotation states).
